Here's how I'd build it. I checked the current APIs first, and Spotify's February 2026 changes shape the plan.

What I found

Fact Effect on the plan
Spotify (Feb 2026) removed browse/new-releases and artists/{id}/top-tracks for apps in Development mode, cut search to 10 results per page, and removed popularity from tracks and artists Spotify can't be the source of "latest songs" by itself; search can only be used sparingly
Development-mode apps are capped at 5 users, and the app owner needs Premium This limits the whole app, not just the recommender. Opening it to more people needs Spotify's approval
ReccoBeats recommendations work. I tested it: seed track → similar tracks, returned with Spotify IDs + ISRC, and it accepted valence/energy targets (the results changed when I set them) This is the main engine: taste (seeds) + mood (targets) in one call, free, no key
Last.fm answered 400 without a key Free API key needed (normal)
Your app already uses Spotify's new /items playlist paths No breakage from the Feb 2026 changes

The core idea: a live "candidate pool" replaces catalog.db

Calling 5 APIs inside every 90-second cycle would be slow and would hit rate limits. So the recommender runs in the background and keeps a pool of a few hundred fresh, personal songs, refreshed about every 30–60 minutes and immediately when the target changes. Each cycle then searches that pool in mood space, exactly as it searches catalog.db today.

             every 30–60 min / on target change  (background)
 ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
 │ 1 taste profile   →  2 gather candidates  →  3 enrich  →  4 filter+score │
 └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                   │
                         candidate pool (~300–800 songs, each with
                         valence/energy, tempo, key, language, release date, taste score)
                                   │
 every 90 s cycle:  Markov waypoint → nearest in pool (mood) + taste score → MMR → insert

The pool has the same shape as today's TrackTable, so Moodly hardly changes. catalog.db stays only as an offline fallback.

The pipeline, step by step

1. Taste profile (refreshed daily)

  • Spotify: your top tracks and top artists (4 weeks / 6 months / all time), recently played, saved tracks. These are still available.
  • Moodly's skip log: which inserted songs you finished or skipped, per artist and per source.
  • Derived: favourite artists, genres/tags, language shares (e.g. Telugu 55%, Hindi 30%), preferred era.

2. Gather candidates (the sources)

Source Gives How
ReccoBeats recommendations (main) taste + mood together seeds = 3–5 of your top tracks (rotated), targets = the current target's valence/energy
Similar artists → their latest songs new-to-you artists in your taste Last.fm artist.getSimilar / ListenBrainz → Spotify search artist:"X" year:2025-2026. This replaces the removed "artist top tracks"
New releases the latest music Spotify search filter tag:new (albums from the last ~2 weeks), plus ListenBrainz fresh releases, filtered to your languages and genres
Your library familiar songs already in Moodly

3. Enrich each song (cached forever per song, in SQLite)

  • Spotify ID: ReccoBeats already gives it. For other sources, use Spotify search isrc:XXXX.
  • Mood features (valence, energy, tempo, key…): ReccoBeats, already in your feature chain. Brand-new songs may not be analysed yet. Fallback: ReccoBeats can analyse an audio file, such as a 30-second preview.
  • Language: the artist's language from their genres/tags ("telugu pop", "filmi"), confirmed by lyrics (LRCLIB, free) → a language detector (lingua-py, good on short text). Labels are cached per artist, so most songs need no lookup.
  • Release date: from Spotify's track/album data.

4. Filter and score

  • Hard filters: language in {top-1, top-2} (or a soft 80/20 mix), not recently played or removed, has valence/energy, explicit setting.
  • Taste score (0–1): favourite artist > similar artist (by similarity rank) > same tags; ReccoBeats seed match; adjusted by skip history.
  • Freshness: a small bonus for recent releases, plus a quota: e.g. at least 25% of inserted songs from the last 6 months.

5. Inside Moodly (small change)

rank_candidates already scores distance to the waypoint plus musical fit. It gets one more term:

score = mood/waypoint distance + musical fit − w_taste · taste − w_fresh · freshness

The rule "must be closer to the target than your current mood" stays, so the mood logic is unchanged.

Tools

Purpose Tool
Language Python 3.13 (same as Moodly)
HTTP httpx (timeouts, retries, Retry-After on 429)
Spotify your existing client (spotipy + the /items fixes)
Taste + mood recommendations, features ReccoBeats (free, no key)
Similar artists, tags Last.fm API (free key), ListenBrainz / MusicBrainz (open, no key)
New releases Spotify search tag:new, ListenBrainz fresh releases
Lyrics → language LRCLIB (free) + lingua-py
Cache SQLite (songs, features, language, artist labels, API responses with expiry)
Maths / ranking numpy now; scikit-learn / LightGBM later for a model learned from skips
Tests pytest + respx (recorded fake API responses, so tests never hit the internet)
Delivery separate GitHub repo moodly-recommender, installed into Moodly with pip from a version tag and bundled into the installer by PyInstaller. No server needed

The contract between the two projects:

rec = Recommender(spotify_client, cache_dir)
rec.refresh_profile()                 # daily
rec.refresh_pool(target=(0.25, 0.20)) # background, ~every 30–60 min and on target change
pool = rec.pool()                     # TrackTable-like: features + language + taste + released
rec.feedback(track_id, skipped=True)  # from Moodly's skip log

Milestones

# Deliver You'll see
M1 Repo, cache, ReccoBeats-seeded pool, plugged into Moodly with catalog.db fallback Songs clearly closer to your taste
M2 Language profile + language filter Only your top languages
M3 Similar artists + new releases (tag:new, ListenBrainz) + freshness quota Latest songs appearing
M4 Taste score + skip-rate comparison (old catalog vs new pool) A number proving it's better
M5 (later) Model learned from your skips; explore/exploit balance Gets more personal over time

Risks

  • ReccoBeats is a free third-party service. It could slow down, change or disappear. Hence the cache and keeping catalog.db as a fallback.
  • The 5-user Spotify limit affects the whole app, not just this part.
  • Language detection for Indian songs with English-lettered titles relies on artist labels and lyrics, and won't be perfect. Expect a few misses, and let skips correct them.
  • Spotify's terms forbid training ML models on Spotify data. Milestones M1–M4 use rules and lookups. For M5, train on non-Spotify signals or check the terms first.
  • The Last.fm API key would be inside the installer. That's normal for Last.fm keys, but each user's PC makes its own calls.

If you agree with this plan, I'll start M1: create the moodly-recommender repo (private, under msja1729, with main/dev like Moodly) and build the ReccoBeats pool and cache, with a dev-branch hook in Moodly so you can try it.

Sources: - Spotify: February 2026 Web API Dev Mode Changes – Migration Guide - Spotify: Web API Changelog – July 2026 - TechCrunch: Spotify changes developer mode API to require premium accounts, limits test users - rspotify issue #550: Spotify Web API changes (February 2026) - ReccoBeats Track API (apis.io) - ListenBrainz API docs: core and recommendation

User
Why to download the database. Their Database updates frequently right? So why cant we make an API call to get the details of the songs and for the recommendation?

1 image(s) attached

Claude's response

You're right, and the plan already does that: nothing gets downloaded. Every song comes from live API calls to ReccoBeats, so new releases arrive as soon as ReccoBeats adds them. What I called the "pool" is only a short-term memory of recent API answers, not a downloaded database. The only downloaded data is today's catalog.db, and the plan keeps it just as an emergency fallback.

Why not call the API for every song pick?

You can, but a small cache in front of the calls makes it work better. ReccoBeats is a free service, and the music shouldn't depend on every call succeeding.

Reason What happens without a cache
One cycle needs several calls: recommendations (song list), then audio features (valence, energy, tempo) for those songs, plus language info from elsewhere Each cycle waits on 2–4 network calls in a row
Rate limits: ReccoBeats answers 429 Too Many Requests when called too often Busy moments (session start, mood change) would get throttled
Outages: a free service can be slow or down No new songs, or the queue runs dry. With a cache, the music continues
The same songs come back: recommendations for similar seeds overlap a lot Asking again for features you already have wastes calls

A song's valence or language never changes, so caching it is safe. The list of recommendations is what should stay fresh, and that's the part we call live.

Better design: call it every cycle, seeded by the current queue

Your question points to a better design than my "refresh every 30–60 minutes" plan. ReccoBeats takes seed songs + mood targets, which is exactly a cycle's situation:

every cycle (90 s):
  seeds   = 3–5 songs from the current window + your top tracks   ← taste
  targets = the Markov waypoint (valence, energy)                  ← mood
  GET /v1/track/recommendation?seeds=…&valence=…&energy=…&size=50  ← live, fresh
  → features for songs not yet in the cache (one batched call)
  → language filter, taste score → Moodly's mood filter + MMR → insert

That's 1–2 calls per cycle, at most once every 30 seconds. That's light, and the recommendations follow your current music, step by step toward the target. The cache only remembers song details, and serves as the fallback when a call fails.

Two things the API won't do for us

  1. Taste in Spotify's sense. ReccoBeats says it recommends "based on the characteristics of the song": songs that sound similar to the seeds, not songs that people who like the seeds also like. In my test, a pop seed returned 90s R&B and emo rock, which sound alike but are a different crowd. That's why the plan adds similar artists (Last.fm or ListenBrainz) and your skip history.
  2. Language. Its responses have no language field, so a Telugu seed may return English songs that sound similar. The language filter still needs its own lookup, done once per artist and cached.

"Updated regularly" is their claim, but how quickly brand-new releases appear is unknown. Adding Spotify's tag:new search covers the newest songs for sure.

Before building anything, I'd run a quick test: seed ReccoBeats with 5 of your top songs and see how many results match your taste and languages. That shows how much of the work ReccoBeats can do alone and how much the extra sources need to cover. Shall I run it?

Song recommendations now match your taste, mood and top-2 languages. Everything is committed to dev and pushed; main is unchanged for you to merge. All 218 tests pass. I haven't run it inside a real listening session yet, only read-only checks against your account.

How a song is chosen now: - Taste: your favourite artists come from your Spotify top tracks plus Moodly's own play and skip log. Their similar artists come from Deezer. Songs by artists you skip in Moodly count for less. - Languages: your top 2 come from what you've listened to recently. For you that's Telugu 52% and Hindi 33%. Punjabi (10%) and English (3%) are left out because a second language needs at least 15%. - New releases: each artist is also searched for songs from the last two years, and recent songs get a small bonus. - Mood: the existing mood-step rules are unchanged. If a song has no mood data, a 30-second iTunes preview is analysed to get it. - No downloaded database: songs come from live lookups and are cached, so the pool survives restarts and keeps growing. The old song catalog is only a fallback.

Changes from my earlier plan, based on tests with your account: - ReccoBeats recommendations are dropped. It knew only 10 of your top 25 songs, and a Hindi song as a starting point returned English and Portuguese songs. Deezer's similar artists are used instead. - Spotify no longer gives artist genres, so a song's language is worked out from album markers like [TELUGU], then the script of the title or lyrics, then romanized lyric words, then the artist's usual language. - No new Spotify permission: "recently listening" uses Moodly's own play log, so you don't need to sign in again.

Turn 4511 Oct 2026, 22:09 IST
User
So basically it now adds the songs into the playlist which are close to my taste, mood and moves towards the Target mood right?
Claude's response

Yes, with a couple of caveats.

What each added song meets:

Requirement How
Moves toward the target It must be closer to the target than your current mood, and near the next Markov step. This rule is unchanged.
Fits the mood step It's ranked by distance to that step, plus smooth tempo, key and loudness against the songs around it.
Your taste It's taken from the taste pool (your favourite artists and their similar artists) and scores higher the closer it is to your favourites.
Your languages Only Telugu or Hindi, your current top 2.
Variety No repeated artists, and no near-duplicate songs.

When it falls back to other songs: 1. At the very start: for about the first 1.5 minutes, before the first refresh finishes. During that time songs come from the old catalog, in any language and not matched to your taste. 2. When the pool has nothing near the next mood step: it first allows a slightly bigger step from your taste pool. If even that doesn't work, it uses a catalog song for that step. You'll see using the catalog in data/daemon.log when this happens. As the pool grows each refresh, this gets rarer.

One thing to expect: the mood still changes gradually. Your mood is the average of about 20 queued songs, so it shifts as new songs are added and older ones play. Reaching a target that's far away takes a while, just as before. The new part is that the songs carrying you there are ones you'd actually like.

So the next real test is a live session. Play music, pick a target mood, and check that the added songs feel like your taste.

Turn 4611 Oct 2026, 22:10 IST
User
What is this. I didnot get you?

1 image(s) attached

Claude's response

Picture the app as a shop assistant who picks songs for you from two shelves:

SHELF 1: your taste pool    songs by your favourite + similar artists, Telugu/Hindi only
                            (built by the recommender, grows over time: 36 → 66 → 86 → …)

SHELF 2: the old catalog    ~90,000 random songs from a public dataset
                            (any language, not chosen for you, but covers every mood)

Normally every song comes from shelf 1. There are two moments when shelf 1 can't help, and the assistant takes from shelf 2 so your music doesn't stop or stall.

1. Right after you start the app

Shelf 1 has to be filled first. The recommender reads your listening, finds artists, checks languages and analyses moods. That takes about 1.5 minutes in the background, and until it's done shelf 1 is empty. If the app needs to add songs during those first 1.5 minutes, it takes them from shelf 2: random songs that fit the mood, but not necessarily your taste or language.

2. When no song in your pool fits the next step

Each cycle needs a song with a specific mood. Say your queue is very energetic and you choose Calm. The next step needs a song that's "a little calmer than now". If your 86-song pool has no song with roughly that mood: - First try: a song from your pool that's a bit further off, calmer than ideal but still your taste. - If even that doesn't exist: one song for this step from shelf 2.

The more songs in your pool, the more moods it covers, so this happens less and less.

Two ways I could reduce this

  1. Fix the start (#1) almost completely. Your pool is saved on disk, but right now the app rebuilds it before using it, so every restart waits 1.5 minutes. I can make it load the saved pool instantly at startup. Then shelf 2 is used at the start only on the very first launch ever.
  2. Never use shelf 2 for mood steps (#2). When your pool has nothing suitable, the app would add nothing that cycle and wait for the pool to grow, instead of adding a random song. The trade-off: your mood moves more slowly, but every added song is always your taste and language.

Do you want either or both? I'd recommend doing 1 in any case. For 2, it's your choice: is it better that every added song matches your taste, or that the mood keeps moving?

Turn 4711 Oct 2026, 22:13 IST
User
What is this pool concept Explain me this and then I will tell what to do with those two ways and which you should do understood
Claude's response

The pool is a ready-made shortlist of songs that the app has already checked and found right for you. When it needs to add a song, it picks from this shortlist.

What's in it

Each song in the pool has passed three checks in advance:

Check Example
✅ Your taste: by one of your favourite artists, or an artist similar to them Pritam, Thaman S, Sid Sriram… or similar artists like Devi Sri Prasad
✅ Your language: Telugu or Hindi Tamil and English songs are left out
✅ Mood known: its valence and energy are measured e.g. Samjhawan → valence 0.52, energy 0.48

Each song also carries a taste score (how close it is to your favourites) and a freshness score (how new it is).

It doesn't have to match a particular mood. The pool holds songs of all moods (calm ones, energetic ones, sad ones), all in your taste.

Why have a pool at all?

Checking a song takes time. To approve one song, the app must: 1. find it on Spotify (search by artist) 2. work out its language (fetch the lyrics, look at the script) 3. measure its mood, often by downloading a 30-second preview and having it analysed

That's several seconds per song, across several different websites. The app picks songs every ~90 seconds and needs answers instantly. So the slow checking is done beforehand, in the background, and the results are kept in the pool. When it's time to add a song, the choice is instant.

It's like a restaurant kitchen: the vegetables are washed and chopped before the dinner rush (that's the pool). When an order comes in, the cook just picks what's ready.

How the pool is built and grows

every 30 minutes, in the background (~1.5 min of work):
  1. read your listening        → favourite artists, top languages
  2. pick a few artists         → favourites + similar artists (8 per round)
  3. find their songs           → Spotify search, including new releases
  4. check language             → keep only Telugu / Hindi
  5. measure mood               → valence + energy
  6. approved songs → added to the pool

Each round checks only a few artists, so the pool grows step by step. In your test it went 36 → 66 → 86 songs over three rounds. Everything checked is saved on disk (data/recommender.db), so no song is ever checked twice.

How it's used when adding a song

Each cycle the app knows the mood it needs next (the next step toward your target). It looks in the pool:

Needed: a song "a bit calmer than now", e.g. valence 0.55, energy 0.55
Pool:   86 songs of all moods, all your taste
  →  keep those that move you toward the target
  →  pick the ones closest to (0.55, 0.55)
  →  among similar ones, prefer higher taste (Pritam > a similar artist)
  →  add 1–3 of them to the playlist

Where the two fallback cases come from

  • The pool is empty at the very start. Until the first background round finishes (~1.5 min), there's nothing to pick from.
  • The pool has no song of the needed mood. With only 86 songs, some moods have few or no songs. For example, there may be no very calm Telugu song by your artists yet.

In both cases the app currently borrows a song from the old ~90,000-song catalog.

So: - Way 1 means using the saved pool right away at startup instead of waiting for the first round. - Way 2 means that when the pool has no song for the needed mood, the app waits instead of borrowing from the old catalog.

The session buttons and the per-session song pool are in, and the songs it adds are now always from your taste. Everything is committed and pushed to dev (main is untouched), and all 222 tests pass. I haven't tried it in a live listening session yet.

Sidebar buttons: - At launch only Start session shows, and nothing is touched until you click it. - During a session it shows End session and New session instead. They replace the old "Pause nudging" toggle. - End session gives your next 10 songs back to your normal queue, deletes the session playlist and clears the song pool from disk. - New session does the same, then starts a fresh session with a new pool.

Waiting until the pool is ready: - When it waits: after Start session, after New session, and every time you change the target mood. - What happens meanwhile: your own queued songs keep playing and nothing is added or removed. The pool is filled in the background, and the sidebar shows "Preparing songs for you in Telugu, Hindi (7/15 ready)". - When it starts adding: once the pool has 15 songs that move your mood toward the target. - On a target change: songs that would pull you away from the new target are taken out of the pool, and artists whose songs already have that mood are searched first.

No more random catalog songs: if your pool has nothing close to the next mood step, that update adds nothing and the pool is grown first.

Two choices I made where your instructions left a gap (tell me if you want them different): 1. Keeping the music going. If your queue is about to run out while it's waiting (fewer than 2 songs left), it adds 1–2 songs anyway, taken from your pool or your own top songs. Without this, Spotify would switch to autoplay, and the app would read that as you starting other music and end the session. 2. A 10-minute limit on waiting. If your taste has few songs for the target mood, it stops waiting after 10 minutes and adds from what it has. Without a limit it could wait forever.

What's cleared when a session ends: only the song pool (data/recommender.db). The cache of mood measurements is kept: it's not part of the pool, it's also used for your own queue songs, and it makes building the next session's pool faster.

One cost of your design: every session builds its pool from scratch, so the first few minutes of each session are spent preparing. Each round of pool-building took about 1–1.5 minutes in the earlier test. Lowering ready_songs in the config starts the adding sooner.

Docs: - SPEC.md.md: the session buttons, the preparing phase, the target-specific pool, no catalog songs for mood steps, and clearing the pool at session end, with matching acceptance criteria. - MODIF.md: M14 is now a row in the summary table, and this change is written up in detail as M15, tagged 🆕 LATEST. - README, the manual and LOG.md are updated to match.

To try it, run python -m frontend.app, play music in Spotify, pick a mood, then click Start session and watch the preparing message and then the songs being added.

Turn 4911 Oct 2026, 22:46 IST
User
Note that the language it shows as a taste, It shows based on the user right? for example another user might have some other languages. Will it show dynamically to them? or not
Claude's response

Yes. The languages are worked out for each user, from their own listening. Nothing about Telugu or Hindi is built into the app. Telugu and Hindi appear because they're what your account listens to.

How it decides, for whoever is signed in: 1. At Start session, it reads the signed-in account's own Spotify top tracks (last ~4 weeks and ~6 months), plus the songs that user played or skipped in Moodly. 2. It detects the language of those songs and computes the shares. Yours came out as Telugu 52%, Hindi 33%. 3. The top language always counts, and the second counts if it has at least 15%.

Examples: - A user who mostly plays Tamil and English gets "Tamil, English" and Tamil/English songs. - A user who listens almost only to Spanish gets just "Spanish", because a second language needs at least 15%.

The sidebar shows that user's languages ("Preparing songs for you in Tamil, English…").

It also follows changes: - Each session builds the profile fresh and clears it at the end, so if someone's listening shifts, the next session reflects it. - Different accounts on the same PC each get their own languages. Logging out ends the session and clears the pool, and the next session reads the newly signed-in account.

The setting that controls this is recommender.languages in the config, which defaults to "auto". If someone sets it to a fixed list (e.g. ["te", "hi"]), that list applies to everyone on that computer, so leave it on "auto" for other users.

One real gap, for languages written in Latin letters: - Scripts like Telugu, Hindi, Tamil, Kannada, Malayalam, Bengali, Korean, Japanese, Chinese, Arabic and Russian are recognised from the script itself, so they work for anyone. - For lyrics written in Latin letters, the app recognises only English, Spanish, Portuguese, plus romanized Hindi, Telugu, Tamil and Punjabi. - French, German, Italian, Dutch, Indonesian, Tagalog and other Latin-script languages aren't recognised yet. Their songs would show as "language unknown" and be left out, so a French listener might see only "English" or no languages at all.

If you want, I can close that gap by adding a small language-detection library (e.g. langdetect) for Latin-letter lyrics. That way the language part works properly for any user, not just ones like you.

How installing differs on a Mac: a .dmg is the Mac's usual installer format, but it doesn't run a Next → Next → Install wizard like Windows. When the user opens it, a window shows the app and an Applications folder, and the user drags the app into Applications. Most Mac apps install this way (Spotify, Chrome, Zoom), so Mac users will recognise it.

Windows:  click Setup.exe  →  Next → Install → Finish
Mac:      open .dmg        →  drag "Ambient Mood" onto "Applications"  →  done

If you'd prefer a Windows-style wizard on Mac too, I can also build a .pkg installer (Mac's step-by-step installer). It's a small addition to the build script.

Two things that still apply: - The repo is private. Only you, and people you invite to the repo, can open the release page and download these files, on Windows or Mac. Make the repo public, or send people the files directly. - First launch on a Mac. Because the app isn't signed with an Apple developer account ($99/year), macOS blocks the first launch. The user allows it once in System Settings → Privacy & Security → Open Anyway. The release notes explain this.

Both make sense. The square edges come from the Windows blur effect: it fills the window's full rectangle, while the glass is only painted rounded, so square corners of blur show behind the rounded ones. And the glass is painted too opaque (about 70–85% solid), which hides most of the blur.

Claude's response

Here are the mouse effects I'd suggest, inspired by modern websites but kept subtle, so the sidebar stays calm.

Recommended (subtle, "premium" feel)

1. Light that follows the cursor (spotlight). A soft glow, slightly tinted with the mood colour, follows your pointer across the glass, as if light were shining on it. When the pointer leaves, it fades out. This is the signature effect on many glass websites (Apple, Linear, Vercel).

2. Edge shine. The thin border brightens on the side nearest the pointer, so the edge seems to catch the light as you move.

3. Hover glow on rows. - When you hover a mood or session row, its glass pill fades in smoothly instead of popping. - A faint light sweep follows your pointer inside the pill. - The icon nudges a few pixels to the right.

4. Click ripple. Clicking a row sends a soft light ripple out from the point you clicked, the way Material-style buttons do.

5. Selection slide. When you pick a different mood, the white bar and the glass pill slide from the old row to the new one instead of jumping.

Optional (more playful; say if you want any)

6. 3D tilt (parallax). The whole panel tilts very slightly toward the pointer, like a card under a light. It looks great, but it's the most noticeable effect.

7. Mood map interaction. Hovering over the mood map shows a small crosshair with the mood under the pointer, e.g. "happy · energetic". Your mood dot gently pulses.

8. Floating particles. Tiny light specks drift slowly inside the glass and move away from the cursor.

How I'd keep it smooth

  • Effects only run while the pointer is over the sidebar. When it's still or away, nothing animates, so no CPU use in the background.
  • Everything glides with easing (about 150–250 ms), and the tint stays the mood colour, so the effects match your current mood.

My recommendation: 1–5, plus 7 (useful as well as pretty). Skip 6 and 8 unless you want it more playful.

Tell me which numbers you want, or "go with your recommendation", and I'll start.

Could you explain me this analysis. Also tell me what is the markov points and how the songs are added into the playlist? Explain me this analysis

2 image(s) attached

Claude's response · 6 tool calls

I'll read the actual trace and the daemon log, so the explanation matches what happened rather than guesses from the screenshot.

6 tool calls
  • Bash: Dump the trace events in readable form
  • Bash: Dump the remaining trace and the session log lines
  • Bash: Check repeat/shuffle handling
  • Bash: Measure progress from when the current target was set
  • TaskStop:
  • Bash: Restart the live mood trace viewer

This run did move your mood toward the target. You set Energized (0.80, 0.85) and the mood ended at (0.77, 0.78). That's a distance of 0.08, or about 82% of the gap closed in roughly 12 minutes. The run also shows a bug in the session: the dip to the left, and most of the 40 songs removed (against 24 added), aren't real mood changes.

What a Markov point (waypoint) is

The mood map is a 10×10 grid of cells, each 0.1 wide. On each cycle the app: 1. Measures your mood: the average (valence, energy) of the song playing now plus the songs coming up. 2. Takes one step on the grid to a neighbouring cell. The Markov chain chooses the direction and favours steps toward the target, but it doesn't jump straight there. That's what makes the change gradual. 3. Sets the waypoint at that next cell (the blue diamonds). When the target's own cell is next, the waypoint is the target itself.

From your run:

Time Mood Step direction Waypoint
12:04:58 (0.48, 0.42) ↗ (more valence and energy) (0.55, 0.55)
12:09:19 (0.62, 0.69) ↑ (more energy) (0.65, 0.75)
12:10:37 (0.68, 0.72) → (more valence) (0.75, 0.75)
12:11:37 (0.73, 0.77) ↗ (0.80, 0.85), the target

How songs are added

  1. Pick candidates: songs from your pool (your taste in your top two languages, here Telugu 55% and Hindi 31%) that lie near the waypoint.
  2. Rank them: - closeness to the waypoint - how well each fits the songs around it (tempo, key, loudness) - your taste and how fresh the song is - variety, so it doesn't pick the same artist three times
  3. Insert each one where it fits most smoothly in the upcoming list, not just at the end. At 12:09 the songs went in at positions 9, 3 and 3.
  4. Remove upcoming songs that pull the mood away from the path.
  5. "Keep going" songs (yellow dots): if fewer than a few songs are left ahead, it adds songs near the target at the end so the music never stops.

How your session went

  1. 12:02:20: the session started with Focused as the target, and your mood was already there (distance 0.01). That's the first point on the distance chart.
  2. 12:02:30: you switched to Energized, so the distance jumped to about 0.46.
  3. Preparing (12:02–12:05): it only measured and added nothing while it built your pool: 40 songs after about 2½ minutes.
  4. 12:05 to 12:12, the real climb: 0.48/0.42 → 0.62/0.69 → 0.68/0.72 → 0.73/0.77 → 0.78/0.80. On the valence and energy chart, both lines rise from about 0.45 to about 0.77.
  5. 12:12 onward: it settled within 0.06–0.12 of the target. Energy stayed a little under 0.85 because your pool has few songs that energetic. At 12:12:31 it reported "waiting for more songs" for that reason.
  6. 12:17:22: you ended the session.

What isn't real

1. "−771% closed" was a bug in my viewer. It measured from the very first point, when the target was still Focused. I've fixed it to measure from when the current target was set. Refresh the page and it shows about 82%.

2. The dip at around 5.7 minutes, to (0.20, 0.37), is a session bug. - At 12:07:45 the session saw only the song playing (Mehabooba) and none of the upcoming songs. So the "mood" was just that one song, and the waypoint (0.25, 0.35) was worked out from the wrong point. - It then added 8 "keep going" songs near the target, which is why the next point jumps up to (0.62, 0.69). - The same thing happened at 12:14:59.

3. Too many songs were removed (40), including songs it had just added. - At 12:13:24 it removed 12 good songs (First Class, Tu Jaane Na, Mast Magan…). - At 12:16:32 it removed 7 of the 8 songs it added at 12:14:59. - In those cycles, the list of upcoming songs it read contains the playlist twice, with songs that already played (Ney Veyrey, Hookah Bar) in between. That's what Spotify's queue looks like when Repeat is on: after the last song it lists the playlist again from the top. - The session doesn't handle Repeat. It reads that second copy as songs you queued yourself, treats the playlist songs as duplicates and removes them. That empties the upcoming list and causes the one-song readings in point 2. - The trace strongly suggests this, but I haven't confirmed that Repeat was on.

Was Repeat on in Spotify? If so, I can fix it so the session turns Repeat off when it starts (it already does that for Shuffle) and ignores the repeated part of Spotify's queue. I'll wait for your go-ahead before changing anything.

Committed to dev as M22, with SPEC, MANUAL and MODIF updated.

How songs get their mood coordinates

Every song's point is just two numbers from 0 to 1: - Valence: how positive or happy it sounds. - Energy: how intense it is.

Neither is adjusted. The app looks them up in this order: 1. Its own cache. A song looked up once is never looked up again. 2. Spotify. This always fails for this app (Spotify blocks the request), so it's switched off after the first try. 3. ReccoBeats, a free online database, looked up by the song's Spotify ID. 4. data/tracks.csv, a local dataset of about 90,000 songs. 5. Last resort: it finds the song's 30-second preview on iTunes and has ReccoBeats analyse the audio. This is limited to 20 songs per refresh.

If none of these works, the song has no point. It doesn't count toward your mood and can't be removed.

Tempo, key and loudness come from the same source. They never move the mood; they only judge whether a song fits smoothly with its neighbours.

How songs are picked

Your mood is the plain average point of up to 20 songs: the one playing plus those coming up.

Your pool of candidate songs is built from: - your Spotify top tracks plus what you finish or skip in Moodly - your top languages: here Telugu and Hindi (a second language must be at least 15% of your listening) - your favourite artists plus related artists found through Deezer

Only songs closer to the target than your current mood are kept.

Each cycle (every 90 s and on every song change): 1. The Markov step. The map is a 10×10 grid. From your mood's cell, it may only step to a neighbouring cell that is closer to the target. It picks one at random, favouring cells nearest the target and small steps. The centre of that cell is the waypoint. - Example: from (0.48, 0.42) toward Energized, it chose up-and-right 39% of the time, straight up 31% and right 29%. 2. Ranking. Each song gets a score; lower is better: - distance to the waypoint - plus penalties for tempo, key and loudness clashes - minus bonuses for your taste and for fresh songs 3. Adding. It adds about 3 of the best, mixing artists, at random places in the queue. 4. Removing. It removes at most 2 songs that are far from both your mood and the target. 5. Keep-going fill. If fewer than 8 songs are left, it adds songs near the target so the music never stops. These are the "keep going" picks you saw.

Because the mood averages 20 songs, each new song moves it only about 1/20 of the way. That's why the change is gradual.

Weak spots

  • Taste can outweigh closeness. The search radius is 0.08 on paper, but it keeps widening until it has 200 candidates. Your pool is smaller than that, so every song gets ranked, and taste can beat closeness to the waypoint.
  • Preview-based points are less reliable. They come from a 30-second clip, sometimes matched to the wrong song, and are saved permanently alongside the better sources.
  • One bad entry can lose a batch. A single incomplete ReccoBeats record discards the whole batch of 40 songs.

Still open: the Repeat bug from your last session (songs removed by mistake and the one-song mood dips) isn't fixed yet. If Repeat was on in Spotify, tell me and I'll fix it.

So for other users it shows their playlists and liked songs right? apart from the default option
Claude's response · 1 tool calls

Yes. Let me confirm one detail first: what happens to the list when someone switches account.

1 tool call
  • Bash: Check whether logging out restarts the daemon

Yes. Each person sees their own list: the default For you, then Liked Songs with their own count, then the playlists they created (and any collaborative ones). The list is read with whichever Spotify account is signed in on that computer, so nobody sees anyone else's playlists.

  • Timing: the list is read when the app starts, then every 10 minutes. A playlist someone creates while the app is running shows up within about 10 minutes.
  • Playlists they follow: playlists made by other people that a user only follows aren't listed, because Spotify won't let the app read their songs. "Liked Songs" works for everyone.
  • Who can use it: while the app is in Spotify's development mode, it's still only the 5 accounts you've added.

There's one gap, found while checking how account switching works. The chosen source is saved in the settings, not tied to the account. If someone logs out and a different person logs in on the same computer, the setting still points at the first person's playlist. The new person can't read it, so their session would end up with no songs to pick from. The fix is small: reset to For you on log out, and fall back to For you if the chosen playlist can't be read (for example, it was deleted). Shall I add that?

Turn 6812 Oct 2026, 14:22 IST
User
What is the default for you mean?
Claude's response

For you is the app's normal way of finding songs, the one it used before this feature. It's selected unless the user picks Liked Songs or a playlist. Instead of taking songs from a list you made, the app finds new songs for you on Spotify, based on three things:

  1. Your taste - It looks at your most-played songs on Spotify (last 4 weeks and last 6 months), plus what you finish or skip in Moodly. - From those it works out your favourite artists. - It searches Spotify for songs by those artists and by similar artists (found through Deezer).
  2. Your languages - It works out which languages you listen to most, for example Telugu and Hindi. - It only picks songs in your top one or two languages.
  3. The mood you chose - It only keeps songs that are closer to your target mood than your current mood is. - Each time it adds a song, it picks one near the next small step toward the target.

So "For you" can bring in songs you've never heard, though they match your artists, languages and the mood you want. With Liked Songs or a playlist, it only plays songs from that list: no new songs, and any language in the list is fine. It still orders them to move the mood toward your target.

Maybe a clearer name would help in the sidebar, like "Discover (for you)" or "New songs for you". Should I rename it?

Turn 6912 Oct 2026, 14:22 IST
User
My taste, mood means the user's taste, mood...etc right?
Claude's response

Yes. "Your" in my explanation means the user who is signed in, whoever that is. Each person's app works it out from their own account:

  • Taste: their own most-played songs and favourite artists on Spotify, plus what they finish or skip in Moodly.
  • Languages: the languages they listen to most. For you that's Telugu and Hindi; someone else might get Tamil and English.
  • Mood:
  • the target mood is the one they pick in their sidebar (Calm, Focused, Energized…)
  • the current mood is worked out from the songs in their own queue.

So two users get different songs from "For you", even with the same target mood. Nothing is shared between users; each computer builds its own profile from the account signed in there.

Right, only you see it. Users never do. The link (in the tray menu and the sidebar) and the dashboard itself appear only when both of these are true:

So even when this goes into a future release, users won't see it.

Both problems are fixed and committed to dev as M26. All 247 tests pass, but I haven't tried it with your real Spotify yet, because you'd closed the app.

1. Good songs were being removed

What actually happened: your rule (remove only songs far from both the mood and the target) wasn't to blame. A different safety rule was: "don't play a song twice". - Spotify in India sometimes reports a song under a different ID than the one in the playlist, even though it's the same song. Spotify calls this relinking. - Because of that, the app couldn't line up Spotify's queue with the playlist. It decided the whole queue was songs you had queued yourself, so every playlist song looked like a duplicate and got removed. - That's why 13 of your songs disappeared at 14:25 and 14 good picks at 14:31. - With the queue empty, the "mood" became just Pal Pal. That's the big jump away from the target in your graph. - Repeat was off, so Repeat wasn't the cause.

Fixes: - Matching: songs are now matched by title + artists instead of ID. - Safety guard: if Spotify's queue still doesn't line up with the playlist, the app treats it as the playlist and removes nothing because of it. - Duplicates: only songs that have already played count, so nothing plays twice. - One removal rule: a song is removed only if it's far from both your mood and the target. That now applies after a target change too; before, a target change removed songs that were merely far from the new target.

2. Mood = what you're actually listening to

  • The mood is now the average of the song playing plus your own songs ahead: what you queued, or the playlist you were playing when you started the session.
  • Moodly's picks don't count until they actually play. So when Channa Mereya plays with only your songs queued, the mood is based on those songs, not on the recommendations waiting in the queue.
  • To keep the change gradual, the planner still looks at where the whole queue is heading when it chooses the next step. Each new pick therefore goes one step further toward the target, but only what you hear moves the mood.

To try it, run .\tools\dev_dashboard.ps1, start a session and watch the dashboard. You should see far fewer removals, and no sudden jumps when songs change.

Why songs were cut after 1–2 seconds

The log showed the same pattern three times: - 15:17:06 Chammak Challo started → 15:17:09 Moodly added songs → 15:17:11 jumped to Aarzu - the same with Chinni Gundelo and Kalank

After adding songs, the app has to reload the playlist so Spotify notices the changes. It reloaded with "start at position N", meaning the song playing. Spotify's count of the playlist was one ahead of the app's, so it started the next song. Spotify seems to count an entry that it doesn't show to apps.

Fix: - Start at the song itself: the app now tells Spotify which song to continue at the same moment, instead of a position number, so numbering differences can't cut a song any more. - Find the song again: before reloading, the app looks the playing song up in the updated playlist, in case the newly added songs shifted it. - Test that catches it: I added a test that reproduces Spotify's hidden entry. It fails with the old behaviour and passes now.

Why the path took a U-turn

It was the cut. Chammak Challo is a very happy, energetic song (0.94, 0.88) and was lifting your mood. It was cut after 2 seconds, so it dropped out of the average. The next song, Aarzu, has no mood data, so it couldn't count either. The mood fell back to your own lower songs ahead, and the path turned back. With the cut fixed, that won't happen this way.

Another bug I found and fixed

A new session remembered the previous one: what had played, what Moodly had added, what was removed. That's why Lutt Le Gaya and Dhurandhar were removed at 15:15 as "already played"; they had played in your earlier session. Each Start session now begins fresh.

Small dips will still happen

The mood counts the song playing plus your own songs ahead. Here's why it picked songs that "pull away", and the fix. It's committed to dev as M30, with all 257 tests passing. The new version is running for you to test, but I haven't tried it with your Spotify yet.

Why it happened

  1. The first fill all aimed at one small step. Your new Liked Songs session started with only 2 songs ahead, so the app added 11 songs at once, all around the first small step from where you were (about 0.35 valence, 0.55 energy). At that moment your mood was low (0.35, 0.28), so every one of them really was closer to Energized than the mood. Then the mood rose to about (0.43, 0.50). The dashboard compares each song with your current mood, so those first-step songs now show as "pulls away". The queue was a flat plateau instead of a climb.
  2. The mood counted only the song playing. In a fresh session none of the upcoming songs are "yours", so the mood was just the current song. When Chennai Express – Sad Theme played (one of the old songs still in your Spotify queue), the mood dropped to energy 0.03. The next picks then aimed low again: Chitti, Emai Pothane.

Fixes

  • The queue climbs like a staircase: when many songs are needed at once, the app now adds 2 songs per step, each step closer to the target, in playing order. Later songs are always closer to Energized than earlier ones.
  • Stricter picks: every song must be closer to the target than both your current mood and where the queue is already heading. A single low song can no longer drag the picks down.
  • A steadier mood: besides the song playing and your own songs, the mood also counts the last 3 songs you actually listened to (skipped ones don't count). One song can't swing it any more.
  • Shorter waits: Spotify's API was timing out from 16:00 to 16:02, and the app waited up to 24 s between tries. After errors it now retries within 5 s.

The app is running again: the backend started at 16:19 and sees Ay Pilla playing. Clear the old songs from your Spotify queue once, so the Chennai Express leftovers don't interfere. Then click Start session and pick Liked Songs. On the dashboard, the "Up next" list should now show most picks as "pulls toward target".

Before building this, I need to check what Apple Music and YouTube Music actually let an app do. The Spotify version depends on capabilities that the other two may not offer to outside apps: - reading what's playing - playing a playlist from a given song at a given moment - adding and removing songs in a playlist - deleting the playlist - getting each song's mood data

I'll look up their current developer APIs.

4 tool calls
  • ToolSearch:
  • WebSearch:
  • WebSearch:
  • WebSearch:

I've checked, and the Spotify version can't be copied exactly to the other two. It relies on outside apps being allowed to: see what's playing, play a playlist from a given song at a given moment (that's how added songs take effect without a break), add and remove songs, and delete the playlist. Here's what the other services allow:

What the app needs Spotify YouTube Music Apple Music
Official developer access ✅ (what we use) ❌ No official YouTube Music API. Only the general YouTube API, or an unofficial library that logs in with your browser cookies (ytmusicapi, "not supported nor endorsed by Google") ⚠️ Requires a paid Apple Developer Program membership, about $100/year (Apple docs, forum)
See what's playing, Play/Pause/Next ✅ ⚠️ Only via your computer's "now playing" controls (Windows/macOS media controls), not from YouTube ⚠️ Same; macOS also allows scripting the Music app
Play a playlist from a given song at a given moment ✅ ❌ ⚠️ macOS only (Music app scripting); no way on Windows
Add songs to a playlist ✅ ⚠️ YouTube's API allows it, but its daily quota is shared by all users of the app: roughly 200 song additions a day in total ✅
Remove songs / delete the playlist ✅ ⚠️ Same quota limits ❌ No documented way to remove songs or delete playlists (forum, forum)
Song mood data ✅ (by Spotify ID) ⚠️ Possible: match each song by title and analyse a short preview ⚠️ Same, via the song previews

The step that matters most is reshaping your queue as you listen: adding and removing upcoming songs and reloading at the same moment. That's only possible on Spotify, plus partly on Apple Music on a Mac. The "which service?" choice at app start is easy to build; what matters is what the app can do once someone picks YouTube Music or Apple Music.

The app stays Spotify-only, and I haven't changed any code.

I've saved what we found so we don't have to look it up again: - YouTube Music has no official developer access for this. The ways in are unofficial or tightly limited, and none can control playback or start a playlist at a given song. - Apple Music needs the paid Apple developer membership, and gives no documented way to remove songs or delete playlists. - A "lighter mode" is possible for both if you want it later: measure the mood from what the computer reports as playing, offer Play/Pause/Next and the themes, and build a mood playlist for you to play. Live queue reshaping like on Spotify isn't possible there.

Turn 9112 Oct 2026, 22:13 IST
User
For the other user to use my app they should be registered into my spotify developer app right? what are the credentials required and how do I add them
Claude's response

Yes. While your Spotify app is in Development mode, only Spotify accounts you've added in your developer dashboard can sign in to it. The limit is 5 users.

What you need from each person

  1. Their name (any label you like).
  2. The email address of their Spotify account. It must be exactly the email Spotify has on file. If they signed up with Google, Facebook or Apple, ask them to check spotify.com → Account → Edit profile for the email shown there.

They also need Spotify Premium, because the app controls playback.

They don't need any passwords, keys or other details. The app already contains your app's public Client ID and signs in securely (PKCE), so no secret is involved.

How to add them

  1. Go to https://developer.spotify.com/dashboard and log in with your developer account.
  2. Open your app (the one whose Client ID is in config/config.json).
  3. Click Settings → User Management.
  4. Enter their name and Spotify email, then click Add user.
  5. Tell them to install Ambient Mood and sign in with that same Spotify account.

If someone signs in who isn't on the list, Spotify refuses the sign-in, typically with a message that the user isn't registered for this app. The fix is to add their exact email as above.

Good to know

  • Up to 5 users in Development mode. You can remove someone in the same place to free a slot.
  • More than 5 users needs Spotify's Extended Quota Mode. Spotify only grants that to organisations, not individual developers, so plan on the 5 for now.
  • Your own account must keep Spotify Premium, or the app stops working for everyone.

Could you give me the Tech stack of the project in simple language. which is used for what, and why that is chose
Claude's response · 2 tool calls

2 tool calls

  • Bash: Check actuators and CI setup

Here is Moodly's tech stack, grouped by what each part does.

The language: Python

The whole app is written in Python: the window you see, the background service, and the song-picking logic. Why: Python has good libraries for every part we needed: a Spotify library, a desktop UI toolkit, and math. Using one language for everything kept the project simple.

What the user sees (frontend)

Tool Used for Why chosen
PyQt6 The tray icon, the frosted-glass sidebar, the mood buttons, and the Spotify account window It makes real desktop windows that work on Windows and Mac. It can also do see-through glass effects, rounded corners and animations, which simpler tools like Tkinter can't do well.

The brain (backend service)

Tool Used for Why chosen
A background service (backend/daemon.py) Runs all the time, watches what you're playing, and adds songs The work keeps going even when the sidebar is closed. If the service crashes, the UI doesn't freeze.
asyncio (built into Python) Running the regular check-ins, Spotify calls and screen changes at the same time One program can wait on many slow internet calls without getting stuck.
NumPy The math: mood points, distances, and the Markov chain that plans the path to the target mood It's fast and is the standard Python tool for number crunching.
watchdog Spotting the moment you click a mood button, because the UI writes to config.json The service reacts in about 50 ms. The UI and the service talk only through files, so neither can crash the other.

Music data

Tool Used for Why chosen
Spotify Web API through spotipy Reading what's playing, reading your top and saved songs, and managing the session playlist Spotify is the music service the project is built on, and spotipy is the standard Python library for it.
PKCE sign-in Each user logs into their own Spotify account No secret key ships inside the app, so it's safe to hand out to anyone.
ReccoBeats Getting each song's mood numbers (happy or sad, calm or energetic) Spotify blocked its own "audio features" API for new apps in late 2024, so we needed a free replacement.
Spotify Tracks Dataset (Kaggle, about 114k songs) A local backup list of songs with their mood numbers Works offline when the online services fail or are slow.
Deezer, LRCLIB, iTunes Similar artists, lyrics (to detect the song's language), and 30-second previews to analyse unknown songs All free with no key needed. Together they let us find songs that match your taste and languages.

Storage

Tool Used for Why chosen
SQLite Saved song moods, the song catalog, and a log of skipped songs (fed back so the app learns) It's built into Python, needs no server, and is one file on the user's PC.
JSON files Settings (config.json) and live status (state.json) Easy for both the UI and the service to read and write.

Changing your surroundings

Tool Used for Why chosen
Windows gamma ramp (SetDeviceGammaRamp) Slowly warming or cooling the screen colour It's built into Windows, so no extra app is needed.
Editing editor settings Changing your coding workspace theme to match the mood The app edits settings files carefully, keeping your comments and formatting.

Testing

Tool Used for Why chosen
pytest + pytest-qt 261 automatic tests, including button clicks and a fake 10-minute session with network errors We can check every change without opening Spotify by hand.
Developer dashboard + trial reports Live graph of the mood path, with each trial saved as graphs and numbers Proves the app actually moves the mood toward the target. It's only on the dev branch.

Shipping it to users

Tool Used for Why chosen
PyInstaller Turning the Python code into an app, so users don't need Python installed It's the standard tool for this.
Inno Setup The Windows installer (AmbientMood-Setup.exe) Free, and it gives a normal "Next, Next, Install" setup.
GitHub Actions Building the Mac version on GitHub's Mac computers You don't own a Mac, and this is free.
GitHub Releases Where users download the installers Free, with one download page for each version.
Git (dev and main branches) dev for work in progress, main for released versions Keeps unfinished work away from users.

If you need one line in the interview: "It's a Python desktop app. PyQt6 is the UI, a background service does the mood math with NumPy, Spotify supplies the music, free APIs like ReccoBeats supply the mood numbers, SQLite stores the data locally, and PyInstaller with Inno Setup turns it into an installer for Windows and Mac."

Turn 9727 Oct 2026, 10:44 IST
User
Could you tell me how did asyncio use in our app? where it comes into picture.
Claude's response · 2 tool calls

Let me look at how the code uses it.

2 tool calls
  • Bash: Find asyncio usage in the code
  • Bash: Read the daemon main loop and clock

asyncio is used only in the backend service (backend/daemon.py). The PyQt6 sidebar doesn't use it, because Qt has its own event loop.

The problem it solves

The service has several jobs that all run at once: - checking what's playing on Spotify every few seconds - adding or removing songs in the session playlist - noticing when you click a mood button or play/pause - slowly warming or cooling the screen colour - refreshing your list of playlists

Most of these jobs spend their time waiting, either on the internet (Spotify, ReccoBeats) or until the next check is due. Done one after another, a slow Spotify call would hold everything up. For example, a button click would only be handled after the playlist update finished.

asyncio lets one program juggle all these jobs: while one job waits, another one runs.

Where it comes into the picture

1. Starting the service. In daemon.py:838, asyncio.run(amain(args)) starts everything. amain builds the service, applies your mood, then runs the main loop.

2. The main loop (daemon.py:474, run_for). This is the service's heartbeat. Each time round it asks what's due right now: - Spotify check due? It reads what's playing and updates the mood. - Session cycle due? It updates the playlist. - Screen step due? It shifts the colour a little. - Playlists refresh due? It starts that as a separate background job.

Then it sleeps until the next job is due (await self.clock.sleep(...)), so it doesn't use the CPU while nothing is happening.

3. asyncio.to_thread for slow calls. The Spotify library (spotipy) is "blocking": it freezes until Spotify replies. So the service hands each such call to a helper thread and waits for it without freezing: - inference.py:114: asks Spotify what's playing now - audio.py:180: adds a song to the queue - daemon.py:276: runs one playlist update cycle - daemon.py:579: play, pause or next from the sidebar - daemon.py:510: changes the screen colour

4. Reacting to your clicks. The sidebar writes your choice to config.json. The watchdog library notices the change and hands it to asyncio (daemon.py:597): - call_later(0.05, ...) waits 50 ms, because one save can fire several file events and this merges them into one reload. - ensure_future(...) then starts the reload as its own job, so it runs right away even if the main loop is busy.

Play, pause and next work the same way through player_command (daemon.py:565).

5. asyncio.Lock, so two updates never clash. A timer-based update and a "song changed" update could try to edit the playlist at the same moment. The lock (daemon.py:100) makes the second one wait its turn, so the playlist never gets edited twice at once.

6. Fake time for testing. backend/clock.py has three clocks: - RealClock: normal time - FakeClock: sleeping skips ahead instantly. This is how the tests run a 10-minute session in under a second. - AcceleratedClock: time runs 20 times faster, for watching a mock session

Because all waiting goes through await clock.sleep(...), swapping the clock is all it takes.

One thing that is not asyncio

The song finder (recommender) runs in a normal separate thread (daemon.py:385). Building the song pool takes a long time and makes many calls to Deezer, iTunes and ReccoBeats. Keeping it fully separate means the main loop never waits for it; the loop just picks up the results when they're ready.

If you need one line in the interview

"The backend service runs on one asyncio loop that schedules polling, playlist updates, screen changes and button clicks. Spotify calls are blocking, so they go through asyncio.to_thread. A lock stops two playlist updates from clashing, and a swappable clock lets the tests run a 10-minute session instantly."

Also is it the one we are having the sync between the spotify music runnng adn the music bar shown in the sidebar at top?
Claude's response · 2 tool calls

Let me check how the sidebar's music bar gets its data.

2 tool calls
  • Bash: Find how sidebar reads playback state
  • Bash: Read UI watcher, player command, and poll delay

Partly. The sync has two halves, and asyncio runs only the backend half. The sidebar never talks to Spotify itself. Everything goes through the backend service, and the two sides pass small files to each other.

Direction 1: Spotify → the music bar (showing what's playing)

Spotify ──(asyncio loop asks every few s)──► backend service
                                                │ writes data/state.json
                                                ▼
                         sidebar (Qt) notices the file changed → updates song name + bar

Backend side (asyncio): - The main loop calls inference.poll() (daemon.py:497). That uses asyncio.to_thread to ask Spotify "what's playing, at what position, playing or paused?" - It writes the answer into data/state.json (daemon.py:458): song name, progress_ms, duration_ms, is_playing. - Smart timing (inference.py:163): while music plays, it checks every few seconds. It also checks about 0.4 s after the current song should end, so the bar switches to the next song right away. When paused, it checks less often.

Sidebar side (Qt, not asyncio): - QFileSystemWatcher (app.py:348) notices the moment state.json changes and refreshes the sidebar. A QTimer also refreshes it regularly as a backup.

Why the bar moves smoothly between updates

The backend only reports every few seconds, but the bar moves continuously. The sidebar has a 250 ms Qt timer (components.py:995). It estimates the position:

last reported position + time passed since then

When the next real update arrives, it corrects itself. A video player's clock works the same way.

Direction 2: sidebar buttons → Spotify (play, pause, next)

click ► sidebar writes data/player.cmd ► backend notices it (watchdog)
      ► asyncio job player_command(): tells Spotify ► re-checks twice ► writes state.json ► sidebar updates
  • The sidebar writes {"cmd": "pause"} to data/player.cmd (app.py:375).
  • The backend's file watcher starts player_command() as its own asyncio job (daemon.py:565). That job:
  • sends the command to Spotify. If Spotify briefly says "no device", it waits 0.7 s and tries once more.
  • then checks Spotify again after 0.25 s and 0.6 s, because Spotify takes a moment to report the change. Each check rewrites state.json straight away.
  • Instant feedback: the sidebar doesn't wait for this round trip. When you press pause, it flips the button and freezes the bar immediately (components.py:1259). The backend's update then confirms it a moment later.

So, in the interview

"Yes, the backend half of that sync runs on asyncio: polling Spotify at smart moments and handling play, pause and next as separate async jobs. The sidebar is a Qt app, so it uses Qt's own tools: a file watcher to spot state.json changes, and a 250 ms timer to move the bar smoothly between updates. The two sides talk only through small JSON files, so neither can freeze or crash the other."

Turn 9927 Oct 2026, 10:50 IST
User
Give me the major tasks Asyncio does in simple language in short
Claude's response

These are the main jobs asyncio does in Moodly's backend service:

  1. Runs the main loop. It's one loop that keeps all the backend jobs going and sleeps until the next one is due, so it doesn't waste the CPU.
  2. Checks Spotify regularly. It asks what song is playing, where it's at, and whether it's paused. It checks every few seconds while music plays, and again just after a song ends.
  3. Updates the playlist. It adds songs that move you toward the target mood and removes ones that don't.
  4. Reacts to your clicks. When you pick a mood or press play, pause or next, it handles that straight away as a separate job, even if other work is running.
  5. Changes the screen colour slowly. It warms or cools the screen a little at a time.
  6. Keeps slow calls from freezing the app. Spotify calls go to a helper thread through asyncio.to_thread, so the rest of the service keeps working while it waits for a reply.
  7. Stops clashes. A lock makes sure two playlist updates never happen at the same time.
  8. Allows fast testing. A fake clock lets the tests run a 10-minute session in under a second.

One line: asyncio lets the backend do many waiting-heavy jobs at once: watching Spotify, editing the playlist, handling clicks and changing the screen, without any of them blocking the others.

Turn 10027 Oct 2026, 10:51 IST
User
What is this watch dog and how is it different from the Asyncio?

1 image(s) attached

Claude's response · 1 tool calls
1 tool call
  • Bash: Read the watchdog setup code

What watchdog is

watchdog is a Python library that watches files and tells you the moment one changes. It doesn't keep re-reading the file to check. Windows or macOS itself sends a signal like "this file was just saved", and watchdog passes that signal on.

In Moodly it watches two files (daemon.py:526): - config/config.json: the sidebar writes to it when you pick a mood, or start or end a session. - data/player.cmd: the sidebar writes to it when you press play, pause or next.

How it differs from asyncio

watchdog asyncio
Job Notices that something happened, here a file change Organises and runs the work
Question it answers "Did the user just click something?" "What should I do now, and what next?"
Where it runs Its own separate thread, listening to the operating system The main loop of the backend service
Does it do the work? No, it only raises the alarm Yes: reloads the mood, talks to Spotify, updates the playlist

Simple picture: watchdog is the doorbell and asyncio is the person who answers the door. The doorbell only tells you someone is there. The person decides what to do and does it.

How they work together

You click "Calm" in the sidebar
   → sidebar writes config.json
   → watchdog (its own thread) gets "file changed" from the OS
   → hands it to the asyncio loop  (call_soon_threadsafe)
   → asyncio waits 50 ms to merge duplicate events, then runs reload_config()
   → new mood applied, playlist updated

call_soon_threadsafe is the safe hand-over point. watchdog runs in a different thread, so it isn't allowed to touch the asyncio loop directly. Instead it drops a note in the loop's to-do list, and the loop picks it up.

Why not use only asyncio?

asyncio has no built-in way to know when a file changes. Without watchdog, the loop would have to re-read config.json every fraction of a second, which wastes work and is slower. With watchdog, the operating system tells us instantly, so the service reacts within about 50 ms.

One line for the interview: "watchdog detects the file change, and asyncio does the work. watchdog runs in its own thread and hands each event to the asyncio loop safely with call_soon_threadsafe."

Turn 10127 Oct 2026, 10:54 IST
User
Config.json is passed to the backend right? Why did asyncio came and also I donot know why daemon process is there I donot understadn what it does at all?
Claude's response · 1 tool calls
1 tool call
  • Bash: Read how the UI starts the daemon process

Start with the daemon, because the other two questions make sense once that's clear.

What the "daemon" is

"Daemon" just means a program that runs in the background without a window. In Moodly, the daemon is the backend. They're the same thing (backend/daemon.py).

When you open Moodly, two programs run on your computer:

Program What it is What it does
Tray app (frontend, PyQt6) The window you see: sidebar, buttons, music bar Shows things and takes your clicks. That's all.
Daemon (backend) Invisible background program Does all the real work: talks to Spotify, works out your mood, picks songs, edits the playlist, changes screen colour

The tray app starts the daemon by itself when you sign in, and stops it when you quit (frontend/daemon_process.py). You never start it by hand. That's why you've never noticed it.

Why two programs and not one?

  1. A crash in one doesn't kill the other. If Spotify sends something weird and the backend crashes, the window stays open, and the tray app restarts the daemon after 30 seconds.
  2. The window never freezes. Spotify calls can take seconds. If they ran inside the window program, the sidebar would hang. Here the window only draws and clicks, so it stays smooth.
  3. Each side has one job. That makes both easier to build and test.

How config.json fits in

Two separate programs can't call each other's functions directly. So they talk through files, like passing notes:

Tray app (window)                         Daemon (backend)
   you click "Calm"
   writes config.json   ──── note ────►   reads config.json → applies the new mood
   reads state.json     ◄─── note ─────   writes state.json (song, progress, mood)

So yes, config.json is how your choice reaches the backend. The UI doesn't "send" it, though. It writes the file, and the backend notices the change (that's watchdog's job) and reads it.

Where asyncio comes in

asyncio has nothing to do with sending config.json. It works inside the daemon, organising the daemon's own work.

The daemon has many jobs running at the same time: - check Spotify every few seconds - update the playlist - react when config.json changes - handle play, pause and next - change the screen colour slowly

Most of these mean waiting, usually on Spotify's reply. asyncio lets the daemon juggle them, so that while one job waits, another runs, and nothing gets stuck behind a slow Spotify call.

All three together, as a restaurant

  • Tray app = waiter. Takes your order and shows you your food.
  • config.json = order slip. The waiter writes it and the kitchen reads it.
  • Daemon = kitchen. Does the actual cooking.
  • watchdog = the kitchen bell that rings when a new slip arrives.
  • asyncio = the chef's way of cooking many dishes at once. While the pasta boils, they chop vegetables instead of standing and waiting.

One line for the interview: "Moodly runs as two programs: a PyQt tray app for the UI and a background daemon for the logic. They talk through JSON files: the UI writes config.json and the daemon writes state.json. watchdog tells the daemon instantly when the config changes, and inside the daemon asyncio runs all the waiting-heavy jobs, like polling Spotify and updating the playlist, at the same time."

Turn 10227 Oct 2026, 10:57 IST
User
Watch dog is part of backend? No right?
Claude's response

Actually, watchdog is part of the backend. It runs inside the daemon, in start_watcher (daemon.py:526).

That makes sense: the daemon needs to know when you change something, so the daemon is the one that watches the files.

Frontend (tray app)                         Backend (daemon)
  writes config.json / player.cmd   ──►   watchdog notices → asyncio does the work
  QFileSystemWatcher notices        ◄──   writes state.json

The frontend has its own watcher, but it isn't watchdog. It uses QFileSystemWatcher, a tool built into PyQt6 that does the same kind of job (app.py:348).

Side Watcher Watches Why
Backend watchdog (Python library) config.json, player.cmd To react to your clicks
Frontend QFileSystemWatcher (built into Qt) state.json To update the song, music bar and mood dot

Why two different tools? Each side uses what fits its own world. The Qt window already has Qt's watcher built in. The backend has no Qt, so it uses watchdog, the standard Python library for this.

One line: "Both sides watch files. The backend uses watchdog to catch the user's clicks, and the frontend uses Qt's QFileSystemWatcher to catch the backend's updates."

Turn 10327 Oct 2026, 10:58 IST
User
Qt is part of the PyQT6 library?
Claude's response

It's the other way round: PyQt6 is a part built on top of Qt.

  • Qt is a big, well-known toolkit for building desktop apps (windows, buttons, animations and so on). It's written in C++ and used by apps like VLC, OBS and parts of KDE Linux.
  • PyQt6 is a bridge that lets you use Qt 6 from Python. You write Python, and behind the scenes Qt's fast C++ code does the drawing.
Your Python code (frontend/app.py)
        ↓ uses
PyQt6  (Python bridge)
        ↓ calls
Qt 6   (the real C++ toolkit that draws the windows)

So when I said "Qt's QFileSystemWatcher" or "Qt timer", I meant tools from Qt that we use through PyQt6. In the code they come from PyQt6:

from PyQt6.QtCore import QFileSystemWatcher, QTimer

One line for the interview: "The UI is built with Qt, a C++ desktop toolkit. We use it from Python through PyQt6, which is the Python binding for Qt 6."

Turn 10427 Oct 2026, 11:01 IST
User
What tool of QT is used to design that UI?
Claude's response · 2 tool calls

I'll check what the frontend code actually uses.

2 tool calls
  • Bash: Find which Qt UI tools the frontend uses
  • Bash: Read frontend header on glass effect

No visual design tool was used. We didn't use Qt Designer (the drag-and-drop editor) or QML. The project has no .ui or .qml files. The whole UI is written in Python code, using these Qt parts through PyQt6:

Qt part What it did in Moodly
QtWidgets: QWidget, QLabel, QFrame, QHBoxLayout / QVBoxLayout, QScrollArea The building blocks: the sidebar window, text labels, and rows and columns to arrange them
QPainter (custom paintEvent) Drawing the look by hand: the glass panel, rounded corners, mood buttons, progress bar, the mouse waves. This does most of the design work.
QLinearGradient, QPainterPath, QColor Colour fades, round shapes, and the mood tint that changes colour
QVariantAnimation + QEasingCurve Smooth animations: fades, the selection sliding between mood buttons, hover glows
QTimer Moving the progress bar, the mood dot that pulses like breathing, the effects that follow your mouse
Window flags: FramelessWindowHint, WA_TranslucentBackground No title bar, and a see-through background, so the window can be rounded glass
QSystemTrayIcon + QMenu The icon near the clock, and its right-click menu
Style sheets (setStyleSheet, a few places) Small styling like fonts and text colours, similar to CSS

How the frosted glass works

  • On Windows 10/11: we ask Windows itself for its Acrylic blur (the same frosted effect the Start menu uses), through SetWindowCompositionAttribute. Windows 11 also adds the rounded corners.
  • On other systems: the app takes a snapshot of what's behind the sidebar, blurs it (blur_pixmap), and paints the glass on top.

The look copies the reference image Glass design UI.jpg in the project folder: a mood-coloured tint, a dark diagonal band, a soft highlight and a thin bright border.

Why code instead of Qt Designer?

Qt Designer is good for normal forms with standard buttons and text boxes. Moodly needed glass, glowing animations and mouse-following effects, and Designer can't make those. Custom painting with QPainter gives full control over every pixel.

One line for the interview: "The UI is hand-coded in PyQt6, not built in Qt Designer. Standard widgets handle the layout, custom QPainter drawing makes the glass look, QVariantAnimation and QTimer handle the animations, and on Windows we use the system's Acrylic blur for real frosted glass."

Turn 10527 Oct 2026, 11:02 IST
User
So finally what I understand is that pyQT6 is visualized as a translator right? You write the code in python it translates correspondingly to c++
Claude's response

Close, but one detail is different: PyQt6 doesn't translate your Python into C++. Your code stays Python. Qt is already built as ready-made C++ code. PyQt6 just connects the two while the app runs, passing your calls through to Qt.

Think of a TV remote: - Qt is the TV. The real machine is already built inside it. - PyQt6 is the remote. It has a Python button for each thing Qt can do. - Your Python code presses the buttons.

Pressing a button doesn't rebuild the TV. It just tells the TV what to do. In the same way, when you write:

label = QLabel("Calm")

no new C++ is created. PyQt6 tells the existing Qt code, "make a label that says Calm", and Qt's fast C++ draws it.

Translator (what you said) Binding (what PyQt6 really is)
Is your Python turned into C++? Yes No, it stays Python
Where does the C++ come from? Generated from your code Already exists inside Qt
What PyQt6 does Converts code Passes your calls to Qt while the app runs

One line for the interview: "PyQt6 is a Python binding for Qt. Our Python code calls Qt's existing C++ library through it, so we write in Python but get Qt's speed for drawing the UI."

Turn 10627 Oct 2026, 11:03 IST
User
So they are like python wrappers right?
Claude's response

Yes. PyQt6 is a Python wrapper around Qt. "Wrapper" and "binding" mean the same thing here.

Each Qt class like QLabel, QTimer or QPainter gets a thin Python shell around it. You use the shell, and the real C++ Qt does the work inside.

Other wrappers in Moodly work the same way: - spotipy is a Python wrapper around Spotify's web API. - NumPy is a Python wrapper around fast C code for the math.

One line for the interview: "PyQt6 is a Python wrapper around the Qt C++ toolkit."
